Well, honestly, I wouldn't exactly say I'm the one asking for help, but... well, I’m here because it’s become this whole big thing with my family and my job. My sister, for one, she just will not let it go. She keeps calling and coming over and saying I've gotten way too thin, making all these dramatic comments about how I look. And I could usually just brush her off, because she's always been a worrier, but then it started at work, too. 

I'm in wholesale manufacturing sales, so I’m out meeting clients all the time, pitching accounts, and you know, appearances are a huge part of the job. You have to look put together. But then HR—literally the HR department—actually pulled me into a meeting to say people were concerned about my weight. Which is just crazy to me, because from my side of things, I mean... my suits have honestly never fit me better. They look so much sharper now, like they were custom tailored, you know? I feel like I finally look the part of a top rep instead of just some guy off the street. 

I mean, yes, I know my weight has kept dropping, I'm not blind to the scale, but it really all started back when I went through my divorce. That was just a massive, stressful upheaval, as you can imagine, dividing up the house and the assets and just the whole emotional mess of it all, and the weight just started coming off then. And it just kept dropping, which I thought was a good thing, a silver lining really, but now everyone is on my case about being too skinny and practically forcing me to come sit in this chair so they'll get off my back.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Intentionally? I mean... look, it's not like I have some master plan where I'm trying to look the way my sister thinks I look, but... I guess, yes, I am very, very careful about not letting the number go back up. Because, like I was saying about the divorce, that whole period of my life was just total chaos. I had zero control over anything—my house, my bank accounts, my future. And losing the weight, getting into these sharp suits, it gave me that control back. 

So now, with my job—and you have to understand, in wholesale sales, there is a lot of wining and dining. It's just part of the culture. I'm constantly taking clients out to these heavy, expensive dinners, or, you know, like last month I had this weekend out in Miami with some major prospective accounts, and there's just rich food and drinks everywhere you turn. And I just... I can't let myself go along with it. Because if I come back from a trip like that, or even just after a regular Tuesday night client dinner, and I step on the scale the next morning and it’s even a single pound higher? Just one pound. I get this absolute panic. 

It’s this overwhelming feeling right in my chest, like I’m completely losing control of my life all over again. And my brain just immediately goes into overdrive. I can't even focus on work because I start planning exactly how I'm going to cut back the next day, figuring out exactly what I need to skip or how hard I need to push it just to get that pound back off immediately. So, yeah, even though HR and my family are making this huge, dramatic deal out of it and telling me I need to eat more, I just can't do it. The thought of the scale going up even a fraction just completely freaks me out.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, exercise is tough because I'm practically living in my car, driving from client to client all day, but as far as cutting back on what I eat? Yeah, absolutely. I have my routine dialed in so tight now, mostly just to avoid that exact panic I was telling you about. 

Most days, I mean, I really just keep it to coffee in the morning. I'll grab a large black coffee before my first meeting, and that keeps me sharp, keeps me focused while I'm doing my morning pitches and talking to purchasing managers. And then later, usually between sales calls when I'm back in the car reviewing my notes for the next account, I'll maybe have a protein bar. Just something quick that doesn't feel heavy, something I can eat while I'm on the highway. 

Then at night, whether I'm at home or at some hotel restaurant on the road, I stick to something really, really small. Like, I'll order grilled chicken, but I'm always very specific with the waiter—no rice, no heavy sides, just the plain chicken. Because the thing is, if I actually sit down and eat a full meal, like a normal-sized dinner that people expect you to eat, it instantly feels like I completely messed up the whole day. It’s like all that discipline I had since 6 AM is just ruined, and this heavy, guilty feeling sets in, and my brain starts screaming that I’ve lost control again and the scale is going to punish me for it tomorrow. 

So it’s just... it’s so much easier to just stick to the coffee and the bar and the plain chicken. It keeps the noise in my head quiet, you know? I don't have to worry about fixing anything or cutting back the next day if I just don't mess it up in the first place.

I'll grab a large black coffee before my first meeting, and that keeps me sharp, keeps me focused while I'm doing my morning pitches and talking to purchasing managers. Just something quick that doesn't feel heavy, something I can eat while I'm on the highway. \n\nThen at night, whether I'm at home or at some hotel restaurant on the road, I stick to something really, really small. Like, I'll order grilled chicken, but I'm always very specific with the waiter—no rice, no heavy sides, just the plain chicken. Because the thing is, if I actually sit down and eat a full meal, like a normal-sized dinner that people expect you to eat, it instantly feels like I completely messed up the whole day. It’s like all that discipline I had since 6 AM is just ruined, and this heavy, guilty feeling sets in, and my brain starts screaming that I’ve lost control again and the scale is going to punish me for it tomorrow. \n\nSo it’s just... it’s so much easier to just stick to the coffee and the bar and the plain chicken. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's not so much that I see myself as... like, objectively huge, you know? It's not like I look in the mirror and see a totally different, massive person taking up the whole room. It’s more... it's highly specific. It's all about the details. 

Like, when I'm getting ready in the morning, especially if I'm at some hotel on the road and the fluorescent lighting in those bathrooms is just so incredibly harsh and unforgiving... I'll be standing there in front of the mirror before a big pitch, or even, you know, if I'm trying to get back out there and go on a date—which is a whole other incredibly stressful situation since the divorce—but I'll stand there, and I just completely zero in on my stomach and my face. 

Those are the two spots. I almost can't look at anything else. I'll turn to the side, I'll check my jawline, I'll check exactly how my dress shirt is sitting across my midsection, making sure the fabric isn't pulling even a millimeter. And if I look even a little bit... soft? Like, if I perceive even the slightest bit of roundness in my cheeks or if my stomach isn't perfectly, completely flat under the tailoring? My brain just spirals. 

Because to me, if I look soft, I instantly feel like everyone else is going to see me as sloppy. Or weak. And you have to understand, in wholesale, weakness is just blood in the water. If you walk into a negotiation looking like you lack discipline, how are they going to trust you with a major account? They won't. They'll just think, 'This guy can't even keep himself put together, how's he going to manage our supply chain?' So I'll stand there for, honestly, sometimes twenty or thirty minutes, just analyzing my face and my stomach from every single angle, adjusting my posture, just obsessing over it. Because I have to project sharpness. I just can't let anyone look at me and see someone who's weak or letting himself go, so I have to be absolutely sure before I walk out that door.
=====================================

That was just a massive, stressful upheaval, as you can imagine, dividing up the house and the assets and just the whole emotional mess of it all, and the weight just started coming off then. And it just kept dropping, which I thought was a good thing, a silver lining really, but now everyone is on my case about being too skinny and practically forcing me to come sit in this chair so they'll get off my back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally losing weight or trying to keep your weight low, even if others have expressed concern about how thin you've become?\n\nPatient: Intentionally? I mean... look, it's not like I have some master plan where I'm trying to look the way my sister thinks I look, but... I guess, yes, I am very, very careful about not letting the number go back up. Because, like I was saying about the divorce, that whole period of my life was just total chaos. I had zero control over anything—my house, my bank accounts, my future. And losing the weight, getting into these sharp suits, it gave me that control back. \n\nSo now, with my job—and you have to understand, in wholesale sales, there is a lot of wining and dining. It's just part of the culture. I'm constantly taking clients out to these heavy, expensive dinners, or, you know, like last month I had this weekend out in Miami with some major prospective accounts, and there's just rich food and drinks everywhere you turn. And I just... I can't let myself go along with it. Because if I come back from a trip like that, or even just after a regular Tuesday night client dinner, and I step on the scale the next morning and it’s even a single pound higher? Just one pound. I get this absolute panic. \n\nIt’s this overwhelming feeling right in my chest, like I’m completely losing control of my life all over again. And my brain just immediately goes into overdrive. I can't even focus on work because I start planning exactly how I'm going to cut back the next day, figuring out exactly what I need to skip or how hard I need to push it just to get that pound back off immediately. So, yeah, even though HR and my family are making this huge, dramatic deal out of it and telling me I need to eat more, I just can't do it. The thought of the scale going up even a fraction just completely freaks me out.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you've eaten too much or gained even a small amount of weight, do you find yourself exercising more than usual or cutting back severely on what you eat to compensate?\n\nPatient: Well, exercise is tough because I'm practically living in my car, driving from client to client all day, but as far as cutting back on what I eat? Yeah, absolutely. I have my routine dialed in so tight now, mostly just to avoid that exact panic I was telling you about. \n\nMost days, I mean, I really just keep it to coffee in the morning. I'll grab a large black coffee before my first meeting, and that keeps me sharp, keeps me focused while I'm doing my morning pitches and talking to purchasing managers. And then later, usually between sales calls when I'm back in the car reviewing my notes for the next account, I'll maybe have a protein bar. Just something quick that doesn't feel heavy, something I can eat while I'm on the highway. \n\nThen at night, whether I'm at home or at some hotel restaurant on the road, I stick to something really, really small. Like, I'll order grilled chicken, but I'm always very specific with the waiter—no rice, no heavy sides, just the plain chicken. Because the thing is, if I actually sit down and eat a full meal, like a normal-sized dinner that people expect you to eat, it instantly feels like I completely messed up the whole day. It’s like all that discipline I had since 6 AM is just ruined, and this heavy, guilty feeling sets in, and my brain starts screaming that I’ve lost control again and the scale is going to punish me for it tomorrow. \n\nSo it’s just... it’s so much easier to just stick to the coffee and the bar and the plain chicken. It keeps the noise in my head quiet, you know? I don't have to worry about fixing anything or cutting back the next day if I just don't mess it up in the first place.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or think about your body, do you see yourself as being larger or heavier than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not so much that I see myself as... like, objectively huge, you know? It's not like I look in the mirror and see a totally different, massive person taking up the whole room. It’s more... it's highly specific. It's all about the details. \n\nLike, when I'm getting ready in the morning, especially if I'm at some hotel on the road and the fluorescent lighting in those bathrooms is just so incredibly harsh and unforgiving... I'll be standing there in front of the mirror before a big pitch, or even, you know, if I'm trying to get back out there and go on a date—which is a whole other incredibly stressful situation since the divorce—but I'll stand there, and I just completely zero in on my stomach and my face. \n\nThose are the two spots. I almost can't look at anything else. I'll turn to the side, I'll check my jawline, I'll check exactly how my dress shirt is sitting across my midsection, making sure the fabric isn't pulling even a millimeter. And if I look even a little bit... soft? Like, if I perceive even the slightest bit of roundness in my cheeks or if my stomach isn't perfectly, completely flat under the tailoring? My brain just spirals. \n\nBecause to me, if I look soft, I instantly feel like everyone else is going to see me as sloppy. Or weak. And you have to understand, in wholesale, weakness is just blood in the water. If you walk into a negotiation looking like you lack discipline, how are they going to trust you with a major account? They won't. They'll just think, 'This guy can't even keep himself put together, how's he going to manage our supply chain?' So I'll stand there for, honestly, sometimes twenty or thirty minutes, just analyzing my face and my stomach from every single angle, adjusting my posture, just obsessing over it. Because I have to project sharpness. I just can't let anyone look at me and see someone who's weak or letting himself go, so I have to be absolutely sure before I walk out that door.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F50.00", "F50.1", "F50.8"],
  "note": "The patient's preoccupation with specific body parts (face and stomach), along with the drive for thinness, ritualized eating behaviors, and fear of weight gain despite being underweight, supports the inclusion of Anorexia Nervosa (F50.00). The behaviors also suggest features of Atypical Anorexia (F50.8). The patient does not meet full criteria for Bulimia Nervosa (F50.1) as there is no mention of binge eating followed by compensatory behaviors, although the restrictive behaviors and concern with body shape and weight support its inclusion as a possible differential. Body dysmorphic concerns are more focused on being perceived as weak or undisciplined rather than a general distortion of body image as seen in Body Dysmorphic Disorder, which is not separately coded here.",
  "is_final": false
}

I'll be standing there in front of the mirror before a big pitch, or even, you know, if I'm trying to get back out there and go on a date—which is a whole other incredibly stressful situation since the divorce—but I'll stand there, and I just completely zero in on my stomach and my face. \n\nThose are the two spots. I almost can't look at anything else. I'll turn to the side, I'll check my jawline, I'll check exactly how my dress shirt is sitting across my midsection, making sure the fabric isn't pulling even a millimeter. And if I look even a little bit... soft? Like, if I perceive even the slightest bit of roundness in my cheeks or if my stomach isn't perfectly, completely flat under the tailoring? My brain just spirals. \n\nBecause to me, if I look soft, I instantly feel like everyone else is going to see me as sloppy. Or weak. And you have to understand, in wholesale, weakness is just blood in the water. If you walk into a negotiation looking like you lack discipline, how are they going to trust you with a major account? They won't. They'll just think, 'This guy can't even keep himself put together, how's he going to manage our supply chain?' So I'll stand there for, honestly, sometimes twenty or thirty minutes, just analyzing my face and my stomach from every single angle, adjusting my posture, just obsessing over it. Because I have to project sharpness. I just can't let anyone look at me and see someone who's weak or letting himself go, so I have to be absolutely sure before I walk out that door.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever made yourself vomit or used medications like laxatives or diuretics after eating to avoid gaining weight?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / sales_representative_wholesale_or_manufacturing / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean, I know that's a thing people do, you hear about it, but... no. That just seems like it would be so messy, and honestly, so out of control. And like I said, for me, this is all about keeping things completely under control, keeping it disciplined. 

If I started throwing up in hotel bathrooms or taking pills that make you run to the bathroom... I mean, think about my schedule. I'm driving all over the state, I'm sitting in waiting rooms at these massive manufacturing plants, waiting to get ten minutes with a purchasing director. I can't be worrying about where the nearest restroom is because I took a laxative, right? That would be a disaster. It would completely undermine the whole point of being sharp and professional. 

And vomiting... I just, I couldn't. It ruins your teeth, doesn't it? And your breath. You can't lean across a conference table and pitch a half-million dollar contract if you smell like... well, you know. It's just not practical for my life. So, no. If I feel like I need to manage my weight, I just don't eat the food in the first place. It's cleaner that way. It's just a simple equation—don't put it in, and you don't have to worry about getting it out. You just stick to the coffee, stick to the protein bar, and you don't have to resort to anything chaotic like that. Because chaos is exactly what I'm trying to avoid here. I just need everything to be tight, and predictable, and... and clean. So, no, none of that stuff.

I get this absolute panic. \n\nIt’s this overwhelming feeling right in my chest, like I’m completely losing control of my life all over again. And my brain just immediately goes into overdrive. I don't have to worry about fixing anything or cutting back the next day if I just don't mess it up in the first place.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or think about your body, do you see yourself as being larger or heavier than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not so much that I see myself as... like, objectively huge, you know? It's not like I look in the mirror and see a totally different, massive person taking up the whole room. It’s more... it's highly specific. It's all about the details. \n\nLike, when I'm getting ready in the morning, especially if I'm at some hotel on the road and the fluorescent lighting in those bathrooms is just so incredibly harsh and unforgiving... I'll be standing there in front of the mirror before a big pitch, or even, you know, if I'm trying to get back out there and go on a date—which is a whole other incredibly stressful situation since the divorce—but I'll stand there, and I just completely zero in on my stomach and my face. \n\nThose are the two spots. I almost can't look at anything else. I'll turn to the side, I'll check my jawline, I'll check exactly how my dress shirt is sitting across my midsection, making sure the fabric isn't pulling even a millimeter. And if I look even a little bit... soft? Like, if I perceive even the slightest bit of roundness in my cheeks or if my stomach isn't perfectly, completely flat under the tailoring? My brain just spirals. \n\nBecause to me, if I look soft, I instantly feel like everyone else is going to see me as sloppy. Or weak. And you have to understand, in wholesale, weakness is just blood in the water. If you walk into a negotiation looking like you lack discipline, how are they going to trust you with a major account? They won't. They'll just think, 'This guy can't even keep himself put together, how's he going to manage our supply chain?' So I'll stand there for, honestly, sometimes twenty or thirty minutes, just analyzing my face and my stomach from every single angle, adjusting my posture, just obsessing over it. Because I have to project sharpness. I just can't let anyone look at me and see someone who's weak or letting himself go, so I have to be absolutely sure before I walk out that door.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used medications like laxatives or diuretics after eating to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean, I know that's a thing people do, you hear about it, but... no. That just seems like it would be so messy, and honestly, so out of control. And like I said, for me, this is all about keeping things completely under control, keeping it disciplined. \n\nIf I started throwing up in hotel bathrooms or taking pills that make you run to the bathroom... I mean, think about my schedule. I'm driving all over the state, I'm sitting in waiting rooms at these massive manufacturing plants, waiting to get ten minutes with a purchasing director. I can't be worrying about where the nearest restroom is because I took a laxative, right? That would be a disaster. It would completely undermine the whole point of being sharp and professional. \n\nAnd vomiting... I just, I couldn't. It ruins your teeth, doesn't it? And your breath. You can't lean across a conference table and pitch a half-million dollar contract if you smell like... well, you know. It's just not practical for my life. So, no. If I feel like I need to manage my weight, I just don't eat the food in the first place. It's cleaner that way. It's just a simple equation—don't put it in, and you don't have to worry about getting it out. You just stick to the coffee, stick to the protein bar, and you don't have to resort to anything chaotic like that. Because chaos is exactly what I'm trying to avoid here. I just need everything to be tight, and predictable, and... and clean. So, no, none of that stuff.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say you are currently at a normal weight, underweight, or do you not know?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I mean... if you ask my ex-wife, or if you ask my manager—who, by the way, actually pulled me aside after the quarterly review last month just to ask if I was "doing okay," which was incredibly embarrassing, honestly, because we were right outside the breakroom and anyone could have heard him—if you ask them, they act like I'm wasting away. Everyone is making this huge, massive deal about it. My mom called me last week in tears, literally in tears, saying I looked like a skeleton in the photos I posted from the regional sales conference. 

But from my perspective? I just don't see what the panic is about. I mean, they act like I'm on my deathbed, but I'm still functioning perfectly fine. Better than fine, actually. I'm still jogging three miles every other morning—well, on the mornings I'm not driving out to the eastern edge of my territory before dawn. I'm still working my entire route, I'm still hitting my numbers... actually, I exceeded my quota by twelve percent last quarter, which is exactly why it was so frustrating that my manager chose that moment to bring up my appearance instead of my performance. 

So, yeah, I know I've lost weight. I know the number on the scale is lower than it used to be. But to call it some kind of medical emergency? Or to say I'm dangerously underweight? It just doesn't compute for me. If I was really that sick, if I was really in danger, I wouldn't have the energy to drive four hundred miles a week and close major accounts, right? I wouldn't be able to keep up with the schedule I have. I feel lighter, I feel sharper, I feel like I'm operating at peak efficiency. So, I don't know... maybe technically, by some chart in a doctor's office, I'm underweight, but it certainly doesn't feel like a problem to me. It just feels like I finally have everything under control.

I'm constantly taking clients out to these heavy, expensive dinners, or, you know, like last month I had this weekend out in Miami with some major prospective accounts, and there's just rich food and drinks everywhere you turn. And I just... I can't let myself go along with it. Because if I come back from a trip like that, or even just after a regular Tuesday night client dinner, and I step on the scale the next morning and it’s even a single pound higher? Just one pound. I get this absolute panic. \n\nIt’s this overwhelming feeling right in my chest, like I’m completely losing control of my life all over again. And my brain just immediately goes into overdrive. I can't even focus on work because I start planning exactly how I'm going to cut back the next day, figuring out exactly what I need to skip or how hard I need to push it just to get that pound back off immediately. So, yeah, even though HR and my family are making this huge, dramatic deal out of it and telling me I need to eat more, I just can't do it. The thought of the scale going up even a fraction just completely freaks me out.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you've eaten too much or gained even a small amount of weight, do you find yourself exercising more than usual or cutting back severely on what you eat to compensate?\n\nPatient: Well, exercise is tough because I'm practically living in my car, driving from client to client all day, but as far as cutting back on what I eat? Yeah, absolutely. I have my routine dialed in so tight now, mostly just to avoid that exact panic I was telling you about. \n\nMost days, I mean, I really just keep it to coffee in the morning. I'll grab a large black coffee before my first meeting, and that keeps me sharp, keeps me focused while I'm doing my morning pitches and talking to purchasing managers. And then later, usually between sales calls when I'm back in the car reviewing my notes for the next account, I'll maybe have a protein bar. Just something quick that doesn't feel heavy, something I can eat while I'm on the highway. \n\nThen at night, whether I'm at home or at some hotel restaurant on the road, I stick to something really, really small. Like, I'll order grilled chicken, but I'm always very specific with the waiter—no rice, no heavy sides, just the plain chicken. Because the thing is, if I actually sit down and eat a full meal, like a normal-sized dinner that people expect you to eat, it instantly feels like I completely messed up the whole day. It’s like all that discipline I had since 6 AM is just ruined, and this heavy, guilty feeling sets in, and my brain starts screaming that I’ve lost control again and the scale is going to punish me for it tomorrow. \n\nSo it’s just... it’s so much easier to just stick to the coffee and the bar and the plain chicken. It keeps the noise in my head quiet, you know? I don't have to worry about fixing anything or cutting back the next day if I just don't mess it up in the first place.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or think about your body, do you see yourself as being larger or heavier than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not so much that I see myself as... like, objectively huge, you know? It's not like I look in the mirror and see a totally different, massive person taking up the whole room. It’s more... it's highly specific. It's all about the details. \n\nLike, when I'm getting ready in the morning, especially if I'm at some hotel on the road and the fluorescent lighting in those bathrooms is just so incredibly harsh and unforgiving... I'll be standing there in front of the mirror before a big pitch, or even, you know, if I'm trying to get back out there and go on a date—which is a whole other incredibly stressful situation since the divorce—but I'll stand there, and I just completely zero in on my stomach and my face. \n\nThose are the two spots. I almost can't look at anything else. I'll turn to the side, I'll check my jawline, I'll check exactly how my dress shirt is sitting across my midsection, making sure the fabric isn't pulling even a millimeter. And if I look even a little bit... soft? Like, if I perceive even the slightest bit of roundness in my cheeks or if my stomach isn't perfectly, completely flat under the tailoring? My brain just spirals. \n\nBecause to me, if I look soft, I instantly feel like everyone else is going to see me as sloppy. Or weak. And you have to understand, in wholesale, weakness is just blood in the water. If you walk into a negotiation looking like you lack discipline, how are they going to trust you with a major account? They won't. They'll just think, 'This guy can't even keep himself put together, how's he going to manage our supply chain?' So I'll stand there for, honestly, sometimes twenty or thirty minutes, just analyzing my face and my stomach from every single angle, adjusting my posture, just obsessing over it. Because I have to project sharpness. I just can't let anyone look at me and see someone who's weak or letting himself go, so I have to be absolutely sure before I walk out that door.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used medications like laxatives or diuretics after eating to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean, I know that's a thing people do, you hear about it, but... no. That just seems like it would be so messy, and honestly, so out of control. And like I said, for me, this is all about keeping things completely under control, keeping it disciplined. \n\nIf I started throwing up in hotel bathrooms or taking pills that make you run to the bathroom... I mean, think about my schedule. I'm driving all over the state, I'm sitting in waiting rooms at these massive manufacturing plants, waiting to get ten minutes with a purchasing director. I can't be worrying about where the nearest restroom is because I took a laxative, right? That would be a disaster. It would completely undermine the whole point of being sharp and professional. \n\nAnd vomiting... I just, I couldn't. It ruins your teeth, doesn't it? And your breath. You can't lean across a conference table and pitch a half-million dollar contract if you smell like... well, you know. It's just not practical for my life. So, no. If I feel like I need to manage my weight, I just don't eat the food in the first place. It's cleaner that way. It's just a simple equation—don't put it in, and you don't have to worry about getting it out. You just stick to the coffee, stick to the protein bar, and you don't have to resort to anything chaotic like that. Because chaos is exactly what I'm trying to avoid here. I just need everything to be tight, and predictable, and... and clean. So, no, none of that stuff.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you are currently at a normal weight, underweight, or do you not know?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... if you ask my ex-wife, or if you ask my manager—who, by the way, actually pulled me aside after the quarterly review last month just to ask if I was \"doing okay,\" which was incredibly embarrassing, honestly, because we were right outside the breakroom and anyone could have heard him—if you ask them, they act like I'm wasting away. Everyone is making this huge, massive deal about it. My mom called me last week in tears, literally in tears, saying I looked like a skeleton in the photos I posted from the regional sales conference. \n\nBut from my perspective? I just don't see what the panic is about. I mean, they act like I'm on my deathbed, but I'm still functioning perfectly fine. Better than fine, actually. I'm still jogging three miles every other morning—well, on the mornings I'm not driving out to the eastern edge of my territory before dawn. I'm still working my entire route, I'm still hitting my numbers... actually, I exceeded my quota by twelve percent last quarter, which is exactly why it was so frustrating that my manager chose that moment to bring up my appearance instead of my performance. \n\nSo, yeah, I know I've lost weight. I know the number on the scale is lower than it used to be. But to call it some kind of medical emergency? Or to say I'm dangerously underweight? It just doesn't compute for me. If I was really that sick, if I was really in danger, I wouldn't have the energy to drive four hundred miles a week and close major accounts, right? I wouldn't be able to keep up with the schedule I have. I feel lighter, I feel sharper, I feel like I'm operating at peak efficiency. So, I don't know... maybe technically, by some chart in a doctor's office, I'm underweight, but it certainly doesn't feel like a problem to me. It just feels like I finally have everything under control.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I know what the standard charts say, right? You see those height and weight tables in every corporate wellness packet HR sends out every January, so I have a general idea of what the quote-unquote "normal" or "healthy" range is supposed to be for a guy my height. But as for my current weight... well, it's definitely below that range. I know that. It's just that the weight has kept dropping since the divorce. 

When Brenda and I split up, everything was just... it was completely chaotic. The lawyers, the moving out, trying to keep my head in the game for my clients and maintain my territory while my entire personal life was just completely unraveling. It was a nightmare. And I guess the weight just started coming off then, and it just kept dropping. 

But honestly, the only reason I'm even sitting here talking to you right now is because HR at my company and my sister keep saying I've gotten way too thin. My sister literally threatened to call my boss about it, which is completely out of line and would be a professional disaster, but she just won't let it go. And HR actually scheduled a "wellness check-in" meeting, which is really just corporate speak for cornering you in a room to scrutinize you. 

They're all panicking and making these dramatic statements, but from my side? I just don't see it. I look in the mirror before I head out to a pitch, and I just see that my suits finally fit sharper. I don't have that awful pulling around the buttons anymore, the tailoring actually drapes the way it's supposed to, the lines are clean. I look streamlined. I feel like I'm finally shedding all that excess baggage, literally and figuratively, and getting my life completely dialed in. So they can throw around phrases like "too thin" all they want, but to me, I'm just finally looking the part. I'm sharp. I don't understand why everyone is treating that like it's some kind of crisis.

My sister, for one, she just will not let it go. She keeps calling and coming over and saying I've gotten way too thin, making all these dramatic comments about how I look. And I could usually just brush her off, because she's always been a worrier, but then it started at work, too. \n\nI'm in wholesale manufacturing sales, so I’m out meeting clients all the time, pitching accounts, and you know, appearances are a huge part of the job. You have to look put together. But then HR—literally the HR department—actually pulled me into a meeting to say people were concerned about my weight. Which is just crazy to me, because from my side of things, I mean... my suits have honestly never fit me better. They look so much sharper now, like they were custom tailored, you know? I feel like I finally look the part of a top rep instead of just some guy off the street. \n\nI mean, yes, I know my weight has kept dropping, I'm not blind to the scale, but it really all started back when I went through my divorce. That was just a massive, stressful upheaval, as you can imagine, dividing up the house and the assets and just the whole emotional mess of it all, and the weight just started coming off then. And it just kept dropping, which I thought was a good thing, a silver lining really, but now everyone is on my case about being too skinny and practically forcing me to come sit in this chair so they'll get off my back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been intentionally losing weight or trying to keep your weight low, even if others have expressed concern about how thin you've become?\n\nPatient: Intentionally? I mean... look, it's not like I have some master plan where I'm trying to look the way my sister thinks I look, but... I guess, yes, I am very, very careful about not letting the number go back up. Because, like I was saying about the divorce, that whole period of my life was just total chaos. I had zero control over anything—my house, my bank accounts, my future. And losing the weight, getting into these sharp suits, it gave me that control back. \n\nSo now, with my job—and you have to understand, in wholesale sales, there is a lot of wining and dining. It's just part of the culture. I'm constantly taking clients out to these heavy, expensive dinners, or, you know, like last month I had this weekend out in Miami with some major prospective accounts, and there's just rich food and drinks everywhere you turn. And I just... I can't let myself go along with it. Because if I come back from a trip like that, or even just after a regular Tuesday night client dinner, and I step on the scale the next morning and it’s even a single pound higher? Just one pound. I get this absolute panic. \n\nIt’s this overwhelming feeling right in my chest, like I’m completely losing control of my life all over again. And my brain just immediately goes into overdrive. I can't even focus on work because I start planning exactly how I'm going to cut back the next day, figuring out exactly what I need to skip or how hard I need to push it just to get that pound back off immediately. So, yeah, even though HR and my family are making this huge, dramatic deal out of it and telling me I need to eat more, I just can't do it. The thought of the scale going up even a fraction just completely freaks me out.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you've eaten too much or gained even a small amount of weight, do you find yourself exercising more than usual or cutting back severely on what you eat to compensate?\n\nPatient: Well, exercise is tough because I'm practically living in my car, driving from client to client all day, but as far as cutting back on what I eat? Yeah, absolutely. I have my routine dialed in so tight now, mostly just to avoid that exact panic I was telling you about. \n\nMost days, I mean, I really just keep it to coffee in the morning. I'll grab a large black coffee before my first meeting, and that keeps me sharp, keeps me focused while I'm doing my morning pitches and talking to purchasing managers. And then later, usually between sales calls when I'm back in the car reviewing my notes for the next account, I'll maybe have a protein bar. Just something quick that doesn't feel heavy, something I can eat while I'm on the highway. \n\nThen at night, whether I'm at home or at some hotel restaurant on the road, I stick to something really, really small. Like, I'll order grilled chicken, but I'm always very specific with the waiter—no rice, no heavy sides, just the plain chicken. Because the thing is, if I actually sit down and eat a full meal, like a normal-sized dinner that people expect you to eat, it instantly feels like I completely messed up the whole day. It’s like all that discipline I had since 6 AM is just ruined, and this heavy, guilty feeling sets in, and my brain starts screaming that I’ve lost control again and the scale is going to punish me for it tomorrow. \n\nSo it’s just... it’s so much easier to just stick to the coffee and the bar and the plain chicken. It keeps the noise in my head quiet, you know? I don't have to worry about fixing anything or cutting back the next day if I just don't mess it up in the first place.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or think about your body, do you see yourself as being larger or heavier than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not so much that I see myself as... like, objectively huge, you know? It's not like I look in the mirror and see a totally different, massive person taking up the whole room. It’s more... it's highly specific. It's all about the details. \n\nLike, when I'm getting ready in the morning, especially if I'm at some hotel on the road and the fluorescent lighting in those bathrooms is just so incredibly harsh and unforgiving... I'll be standing there in front of the mirror before a big pitch, or even, you know, if I'm trying to get back out there and go on a date—which is a whole other incredibly stressful situation since the divorce—but I'll stand there, and I just completely zero in on my stomach and my face. \n\nThose are the two spots. I almost can't look at anything else. I'll turn to the side, I'll check my jawline, I'll check exactly how my dress shirt is sitting across my midsection, making sure the fabric isn't pulling even a millimeter. And if I look even a little bit... soft? Like, if I perceive even the slightest bit of roundness in my cheeks or if my stomach isn't perfectly, completely flat under the tailoring? My brain just spirals. \n\nBecause to me, if I look soft, I instantly feel like everyone else is going to see me as sloppy. Or weak. And you have to understand, in wholesale, weakness is just blood in the water. If you walk into a negotiation looking like you lack discipline, how are they going to trust you with a major account? They won't. They'll just think, 'This guy can't even keep himself put together, how's he going to manage our supply chain?' So I'll stand there for, honestly, sometimes twenty or thirty minutes, just analyzing my face and my stomach from every single angle, adjusting my posture, just obsessing over it. Because I have to project sharpness. I just can't let anyone look at me and see someone who's weak or letting himself go, so I have to be absolutely sure before I walk out that door.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used medications like laxatives or diuretics after eating to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean, I know that's a thing people do, you hear about it, but... no. That just seems like it would be so messy, and honestly, so out of control. And like I said, for me, this is all about keeping things completely under control, keeping it disciplined. \n\nIf I started throwing up in hotel bathrooms or taking pills that make you run to the bathroom... I mean, think about my schedule. I'm driving all over the state, I'm sitting in waiting rooms at these massive manufacturing plants, waiting to get ten minutes with a purchasing director. I can't be worrying about where the nearest restroom is because I took a laxative, right? That would be a disaster. It would completely undermine the whole point of being sharp and professional. \n\nAnd vomiting... I just, I couldn't. It ruins your teeth, doesn't it? And your breath. You can't lean across a conference table and pitch a half-million dollar contract if you smell like... well, you know. It's just not practical for my life. So, no. If I feel like I need to manage my weight, I just don't eat the food in the first place. It's cleaner that way. It's just a simple equation—don't put it in, and you don't have to worry about getting it out. You just stick to the coffee, stick to the protein bar, and you don't have to resort to anything chaotic like that. Because chaos is exactly what I'm trying to avoid here. I just need everything to be tight, and predictable, and... and clean. So, no, none of that stuff.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you are currently at a normal weight, underweight, or do you not know?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... if you ask my ex-wife, or if you ask my manager—who, by the way, actually pulled me aside after the quarterly review last month just to ask if I was \"doing okay,\" which was incredibly embarrassing, honestly, because we were right outside the breakroom and anyone could have heard him—if you ask them, they act like I'm wasting away. Everyone is making this huge, massive deal about it. My mom called me last week in tears, literally in tears, saying I looked like a skeleton in the photos I posted from the regional sales conference. \n\nBut from my perspective? I just don't see what the panic is about. I mean, they act like I'm on my deathbed, but I'm still functioning perfectly fine. Better than fine, actually. I'm still jogging three miles every other morning—well, on the mornings I'm not driving out to the eastern edge of my territory before dawn. I'm still working my entire route, I'm still hitting my numbers... actually, I exceeded my quota by twelve percent last quarter, which is exactly why it was so frustrating that my manager chose that moment to bring up my appearance instead of my performance. \n\nSo, yeah, I know I've lost weight. I know the number on the scale is lower than it used to be. But to call it some kind of medical emergency? Or to say I'm dangerously underweight? It just doesn't compute for me. If I was really that sick, if I was really in danger, I wouldn't have the energy to drive four hundred miles a week and close major accounts, right? I wouldn't be able to keep up with the schedule I have. I feel lighter, I feel sharper, I feel like I'm operating at peak efficiency. So, I don't know... maybe technically, by some chart in a doctor's office, I'm underweight, but it certainly doesn't feel like a problem to me. It just feels like I finally have everything under control.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what your current weight is, and whether you know what is considered a normal or healthy weight for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: Well, I know what the standard charts say, right? You see those height and weight tables in every corporate wellness packet HR sends out every January, so I have a general idea of what the quote-unquote \"normal\" or \"healthy\" range is supposed to be for a guy my height. But as for my current weight... well, it's definitely below that range. I know that. It's just that the weight has kept dropping since the divorce. \n\nWhen Brenda and I split up, everything was just... it was completely chaotic. The lawyers, the moving out, trying to keep my head in the game for my clients and maintain my territory while my entire personal life was just completely unraveling. It was a nightmare. And I guess the weight just started coming off then, and it just kept dropping. \n\nBut honestly, the only reason I'm even sitting here talking to you right now is because HR at my company and my sister keep saying I've gotten way too thin. My sister literally threatened to call my boss about it, which is completely out of line and would be a professional disaster, but she just won't let it go. And HR actually scheduled a \"wellness check-in\" meeting, which is really just corporate speak for cornering you in a room to scrutinize you. \n\nThey're all panicking and making these dramatic statements, but from my side? I just don't see it. I look in the mirror before I head out to a pitch, and I just see that my suits finally fit sharper. I don't have that awful pulling around the buttons anymore, the tailoring actually drapes the way it's supposed to, the lines are clean. I look streamlined. I feel like I'm finally shedding all that excess baggage, literally and figuratively, and getting my life completely dialed in. So they can throw around phrases like \"too thin\" all they want, but to me, I'm just finally looking the part. I'm sharp. I don't understand why everyone is treating that like it's some kind of crisis.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  "note": "",
  "is_final": false

I wouldn't be able to keep up with the schedule I have. I feel lighter, I feel sharper, I feel like I'm operating at peak efficiency. It just feels like I finally have everything under control.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what your current weight is, and whether you know what is considered a normal or healthy weight for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: Well, I know what the standard charts say, right? But as for my current weight... well, it's definitely below that range. I know that. It's just that the weight has kept dropping since the divorce. \n\nWhen Brenda and I split up, everything was just... it was completely chaotic. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's not really about reaching a specific number and then stopping. It's... it's more about the direction the number is going, you know? Like, I don't have a final destination weight in mind where I'll suddenly be like, "Okay, perfect, I'm done, I can just relax now." Relaxing is exactly what got me feeling so out of control in the first place during the divorce. 

So, yes, the weight has kept going down, but that's because the maintenance of it... the vigilance of it... that's the part that keeps me sane. I mean, if the scale is even a pound higher—just one single pound—after, say, a mandatory client dinner where I had to eat a few bites of steak so they wouldn't think I'm weird, or if I have a weekend out in Miami for a regional conference and I can't completely control my environment... if I come back and that number ticks up even a fraction, I get this intense, immediate panic. 

It's like this cold rush in my chest, like I'm completely losing my grip on everything. Like all the discipline, all the sharpness I've built up is just slipping right through my fingers. And the second I see that higher number, my brain just instantly shifts into overdrive. I immediately start planning how to cut back the next day. I'll mentally map out my route and figure out how I can skip lunch entirely, or I'll decide I'm only having black coffee until 5 PM, or I'll add an extra mile to my morning run. I have to fix it immediately. 

Because if I let one pound slide, what's next? Five pounds? Ten? Going back to feeling heavy and sluggish and completely out of control of my own life? I can't let that happen. I absolutely cannot go backward. So, yeah, I guess I have kept losing weight, but it's not because I'm trying to vanish. It's because the alternative—gaining even a tiny bit back—feels like an absolute failure. It feels like chaos. And I just can't tolerate the chaos anymore.

So they can throw around phrases like \"too thin\" all they want, but to me, I'm just finally looking the part. I'm sharp. I don't understand why everyone is treating that like it's some kind of crisis.\n\nDoctor: Have you continued to lose weight even after reaching a weight that you considered acceptable or normal for yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not really about reaching a specific number and then stopping. It's... it's more about the direction the number is going, you know? Like, I don't have a final destination weight in mind where I'll suddenly be like, \"Okay, perfect, I'm done, I can just relax now.\" Relaxing is exactly what got me feeling so out of control in the first place during the divorce. \n\nSo, yes, the weight has kept going down, but that's because the maintenance of it... the vigilance of it... that's the part that keeps me sane. I mean, if the scale is even a pound higher—just one single pound—after, say, a mandatory client dinner where I had to eat a few bites of steak so they wouldn't think I'm weird, or if I have a weekend out in Miami for a regional conference and I can't completely control my environment... if I come back and that number ticks up even a fraction, I get this intense, immediate panic. \n\nIt's like this cold rush in my chest, like I'm completely losing my grip on everything. Like all the discipline, all the sharpness I've built up is just slipping right through my fingers. And the second I see that higher number, my brain just instantly shifts into overdrive. I immediately start planning how to cut back the next day. I'll mentally map out my route and figure out how I can skip lunch entirely, or I'll decide I'm only having black coffee until 5 PM, or I'll add an extra mile to my morning run. I have to fix it immediately. \n\nBecause if I let one pound slide, what's next? Five pounds? Ten? Going back to feeling heavy and sluggish and completely out of control of my own life? I can't let that happen. I absolutely cannot go backward. So, yeah, I guess I have kept losing weight, but it's not because I'm trying to vanish. It's because the alternative—gaining even a tiny bit back—feels like an absolute failure. It feels like chaos. And I just can't tolerate the chaos anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel that your concerns about your weight or shape significantly interfere with your ability to function in your daily life, such as at work, in relationships, or in social situations?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / sales_representative_wholesale_or_manufacturing / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I still manage my sales route, but I avoid client dinners and dates unless I can control the menu, so I’ve been turning down invitations and keeping more to myself.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Interfere? I mean... no, not with the actual mechanics of my job, not really. Like I told you, I'm still managing my entire sales route, I'm still out there driving, I'm still closing deals and hitting my numbers. Work is fine. 

But... I guess if you're talking about the social side of things, or relationships, or whatever... well, it has definitely changed how I handle that stuff. It's just that social situations almost always revolve around food, right? Especially in my industry. It's always, "Let's grab a steak," or "Let's discuss this contract over drinks and appetizers." And I just... I really can't do that anymore. I actively avoid client dinners now. Unless I can look up the restaurant beforehand and absolutely guarantee that I can control what I'm ordering—like, knowing exactly how they prepare the chicken, or knowing I can get a salad with absolutely no oil or dressing anywhere near it—I just won't go. 

I'll make up some excuse, you know? I'll tell them I have a conflict with another account, or I have to drive back to the city early, or I have a massive pile of paperwork to catch up on. Which I know isn't great for networking, but sitting at a table with a client while they order a massive ribeye and expect me to do the same... it's just too much anxiety. My chest gets tight just thinking about having to navigate that conversation. 

And as for relationships... dating is completely out of the question right now. After the divorce, my buddies kept trying to set me up, or telling me to get on the apps and get back out there, but what am I supposed to do on a date? You go out for dinner, you go out for drinks. I can't sit across from a woman I barely know and try to explain why I'm only drinking black coffee or eating three bites of plain lettuce. It's embarrassing. They'd think I'm completely neurotic. So, I've just been turning down all those invitations. I tell my friends I'm just focusing on my career right now, which is true, partly, but... yeah, I guess I have been keeping to myself a lot more lately. Just staying in my lane, doing my job, and going back to my apartment. It's just easier that way. There are way less variables to worry about when it's just me.

How can I help you today?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, I wouldn't exactly say I'm the one asking for help, but... well, I’m here because it’s become this whole big thing with my family and my job. My sister, for one, she just will not let it go. She keeps calling and coming over and saying I've gotten way too thin, making all these dramatic comments about how I look. I get this absolute panic. \n\nIt’s this overwhelming feeling right in my chest, like I’m completely losing control of my life all over again. And my brain just immediately goes into overdrive. I can't even focus on work because I start planning exactly how I'm going to cut back the next day, figuring out exactly what I need to skip or how hard I need to push it just to get that pound back off immediately. So, yeah, even though HR and my family are making this huge, dramatic deal out of it and telling me I need to eat more, I just can't do it. The thought of the scale going up even a fraction just completely freaks me out.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you've eaten too much or gained even a small amount of weight, do you find yourself exercising more than usual or cutting back severely on what you eat to compensate?\n\nPatient: Well, exercise is tough because I'm practically living in my car, driving from client to client all day, but as far as cutting back on what I eat? Yeah, absolutely. I have my routine dialed in so tight now, mostly just to avoid that exact panic I was telling you about. \n\nMost days, I mean, I really just keep it to coffee in the morning. I'll grab a large black coffee before my first meeting, and that keeps me sharp, keeps me focused while I'm doing my morning pitches and talking to purchasing managers. And then later, usually between sales calls when I'm back in the car reviewing my notes for the next account, I'll maybe have a protein bar. Just something quick that doesn't feel heavy, something I can eat while I'm on the highway. \n\nThen at night, whether I'm at home or at some hotel restaurant on the road, I stick to something really, really small. Like, I'll order grilled chicken, but I'm always very specific with the waiter—no rice, no heavy sides, just the plain chicken. Because the thing is, if I actually sit down and eat a full meal, like a normal-sized dinner that people expect you to eat, it instantly feels like I completely messed up the whole day. It’s like all that discipline I had since 6 AM is just ruined, and this heavy, guilty feeling sets in, and my brain starts screaming that I’ve lost control again and the scale is going to punish me for it tomorrow. \n\nSo it’s just... it’s so much easier to just stick to the coffee and the bar and the plain chicken. It keeps the noise in my head quiet, you know? I don't have to worry about fixing anything or cutting back the next day if I just don't mess it up in the first place.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or think about your body, do you see yourself as being larger or heavier than you actually are?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not so much that I see myself as... like, objectively huge, you know? It's not like I look in the mirror and see a totally different, massive person taking up the whole room. It’s more... it's highly specific. It's all about the details. \n\nLike, when I'm getting ready in the morning, especially if I'm at some hotel on the road and the fluorescent lighting in those bathrooms is just so incredibly harsh and unforgiving... I'll be standing there in front of the mirror before a big pitch, or even, you know, if I'm trying to get back out there and go on a date—which is a whole other incredibly stressful situation since the divorce—but I'll stand there, and I just completely zero in on my stomach and my face. \n\nThose are the two spots. I almost can't look at anything else. I'll turn to the side, I'll check my jawline, I'll check exactly how my dress shirt is sitting across my midsection, making sure the fabric isn't pulling even a millimeter. And if I look even a little bit... soft? Like, if I perceive even the slightest bit of roundness in my cheeks or if my stomach isn't perfectly, completely flat under the tailoring? My brain just spirals. \n\nBecause to me, if I look soft, I instantly feel like everyone else is going to see me as sloppy. Or weak. And you have to understand, in wholesale, weakness is just blood in the water. If you walk into a negotiation looking like you lack discipline, how are they going to trust you with a major account? They won't. They'll just think, 'This guy can't even keep himself put together, how's he going to manage our supply chain?' So I'll stand there for, honestly, sometimes twenty or thirty minutes, just analyzing my face and my stomach from every single angle, adjusting my posture, just obsessing over it. Because I have to project sharpness. I just can't let anyone look at me and see someone who's weak or letting himself go, so I have to be absolutely sure before I walk out that door.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever made yourself vomit or used medications like laxatives or diuretics after eating to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't do any of that. I mean, I know that's a thing people do, you hear about it, but... no. That just seems like it would be so messy, and honestly, so out of control. And like I said, for me, this is all about keeping things completely under control, keeping it disciplined. \n\nIf I started throwing up in hotel bathrooms or taking pills that make you run to the bathroom... I mean, think about my schedule. I'm driving all over the state, I'm sitting in waiting rooms at these massive manufacturing plants, waiting to get ten minutes with a purchasing director. I can't be worrying about where the nearest restroom is because I took a laxative, right? That would be a disaster. It would completely undermine the whole point of being sharp and professional. \n\nAnd vomiting... I just, I couldn't. It ruins your teeth, doesn't it? And your breath. You can't lean across a conference table and pitch a half-million dollar contract if you smell like... well, you know. It's just not practical for my life. So, no. If I feel like I need to manage my weight, I just don't eat the food in the first place. It's cleaner that way. It's just a simple equation—don't put it in, and you don't have to worry about getting it out. You just stick to the coffee, stick to the protein bar, and you don't have to resort to anything chaotic like that. Because chaos is exactly what I'm trying to avoid here. I just need everything to be tight, and predictable, and... and clean. So, no, none of that stuff.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you are currently at a normal weight, underweight, or do you not know?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... if you ask my ex-wife, or if you ask my manager—who, by the way, actually pulled me aside after the quarterly review last month just to ask if I was \"doing okay,\" which was incredibly embarrassing, honestly, because we were right outside the breakroom and anyone could have heard him—if you ask them, they act like I'm wasting away. Everyone is making this huge, massive deal about it. My mom called me last week in tears, literally in tears, saying I looked like a skeleton in the photos I posted from the regional sales conference. \n\nBut from my perspective? I just don't see what the panic is about. I mean, they act like I'm on my deathbed, but I'm still functioning perfectly fine. Better than fine, actually. I'm still jogging three miles every other morning—well, on the mornings I'm not driving out to the eastern edge of my territory before dawn. I'm still working my entire route, I'm still hitting my numbers... actually, I exceeded my quota by twelve percent last quarter, which is exactly why it was so frustrating that my manager chose that moment to bring up my appearance instead of my performance. \n\nSo, yeah, I know I've lost weight. I know the number on the scale is lower than it used to be. But to call it some kind of medical emergency? Or to say I'm dangerously underweight? It just doesn't compute for me. If I was really that sick, if I was really in danger, I wouldn't have the energy to drive four hundred miles a week and close major accounts, right? I wouldn't be able to keep up with the schedule I have. I feel lighter, I feel sharper, I feel like I'm operating at peak efficiency. So, I don't know... maybe technically, by some chart in a doctor's office, I'm underweight, but it certainly doesn't feel like a problem to me. It just feels like I finally have everything under control.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what your current weight is, and whether you know what is considered a normal or healthy weight for someone of your height?\n\nPatient: Well, I know what the standard charts say, right? But as for my current weight... well, it's definitely below that range. I know that. And HR actually scheduled a \"wellness check-in\" meeting, which is really just corporate speak for cornering you in a room to scrutinize you. \n\nThey're all panicking and making these dramatic statements, but from my side? I just don't see it. I look in the mirror before I head out to a pitch, and I just see that my suits finally fit sharper. I don't have that awful pulling around the buttons anymore, the tailoring actually drapes the way it's supposed to, the lines are clean. I look streamlined. I feel like I'm finally shedding all that excess baggage, literally and figuratively, and getting my life completely dialed in.
